- **Credits:** 30 credits
- **Subject:** Information Systems
- **Level:** Graduate (Master's thesis)
- **Department:** Informatics / Higher Education Institute network

**Research Methodology:**
The study employed a qualitative research approach with a phenomenological paradigm, focusing on the impact of Large Language Models (LLMs) like ChatGPT in higher education. Data was collected through eight semi-structured interviews and fifteen online surveys using Google Forms.

**Target Participants:**
Participants included university-level students and teachers from various fields of specialization, though non-random recruiting limited universal representation.

**Theoretical Frameworks:**
Key theoretical frameworks used were the Unified Theory of Acceptance and Use of Technology 2 (UTAUT2) by Venkatesh et al. (2012), the Human-Centered AI framework, and the Identify, Determine, Ensure, Evaluate (IDEE) framework by Su & Yang (2023).

**Key Research Question:**
The primary research question was: "What impact does a Large Language Model like ChatGPT have on traditional higher education dynamics from an ethical, behavioral, and social standpoint?"

### General LLM & Technology Facts
- **Definition:** Large Language Models are computational systems trained on vast amounts of human-authored material to replicate human thought in writing.
- **ChatGPT Specifics:** Launched in November 2022 by OpenAI as an instruction-tuned text generator, with GPT-3 being the most popular base model and GPT-4 showing promise for educational applications.
- **Functionality:** LLMs predict next words/phrases to generate content using natural language processing (NLP) to analyze human discourse.
- **Applications:** Business, education, healthcare, content creation; problem formulation, research design, data collection/analysis, reviewing/critiquing academic writing, creating personalized tests/quizzes, and generating presentations.

### Educational Impact & Pedagogy
- **Benefits:** LLMs assist with assignment creation, curriculum enhancement, research support, automation (e.g., grading mundane chores), personalized learning experiences, text-to-speech/translation for accessibility, and synthesizing existing internet courses.
- **Risks to Learning:** Overreliance on LLMs can hinder critical thinking, reasoning, analytical capabilities, higher-order thinking skills, and the ability to think independently; it also reduces face-to-face interaction between students and teachers.

### Ethical & Social Considerations
- **Academic Integrity:** Concerns over plagiarism, invasion of privacy, discrimination, prejudice, data protection, and undesired content generation.
- **Bias & Fairness:** Models may perpetuate biases in training data leading to biased research findings and worsening inequities based on ethnicity, gender, or socioeconomic background.
- **Privacy & Security:** AI systems require vast student information creating worries regarding privacy/security; lack of transparency in decision-making processes.
- **Digital Divide:** Paywall models create barriers where those with financial means gain advantages over students who cannot afford premium services or full functionality trials.

### Specific Participant Findings & Themes
- **Academic Integrity:** Concerns over plagiarism rising slightly (57.1%); calls for university-level regulations, creative pedagogical use to mitigate cheating detection difficulties, and distinguishing between brainstorming vs. direct content creation.
- **Accessibility/Inclusion:** Digital divide concerns due to economic barriers; recommendation to utilize free LLM versions; ChatGPT supports over 60 languages aiding diverse learners.
- **Teaching Methodologies:** Beneficial effects include assignment creation and curriculum enhancement; risks involve reduced critical thinking, increased dependency on technology, and need for proactive educator adaptation.
- **Student-Teacher Relationship:** LLMs may reduce personal interactions leading to strained relationships or decreased engagement; AI can help shy students gain knowledge but overreliance reduces effort.
- **Ethical Considerations/Solutions:** Necessity for ethical guidelines, monitoring, institutional policies, training, review boards, and transparency; balancing technology benefits with integrity preservation.

### Survey Statistics & Data Analysis
- **Usage Patterns:** 30.8% reported occasional LLM use; 38.5% indicated extensive or "a lot" usage.
- **Academic Integrity Impact:** Majority (57.1%) selected that the effect on academic integrity is "slightly rising"; only 7.1% acknowledged a "significantly rising" impact; fewer chose "no change."
- **Teaching Practices:** Equal percentage of respondents agreed and disagreed regarding whether LLM usage affects teaching methods.
- **Teacher-Student Dynamics:** 42.9% expressed that integrating LLMs has improved interactions between educators and learners, compared to only 35.7% who indicated it deteriorated the dynamic.

### Specific Participant Insights
- **Participant A:** Suggested innovative pedagogical strategies acceptable to students while maintaining quality; warned against losing effort due to AI input without thinking.
- **Participant B:** Preferred free ChatGPT over university Copilot despite cost considerations (utility); emphasized need for explicit guidelines distinguishing brainstorming from content creation.
- **Participant C:** Utilizes LLMs for research and summarization but notes reference accuracy limitations vs older models; observed teachers prefer traditional methods leading to strained relationships.
- **Participant D:** Noted ChatGPT 3.5 allows all backgrounds in free version (no accessibility worries); emphasized need for skills to communicate effectively with AI.
- **Participant E:** Described LLMs as a "two-sided weapon" offering dishonesty risks and detection opportunities; warned against hackers using LLMs to find vulnerabilities in telecom/energy grids.
- **Participant F:** Prioritized personal integrity over institutional policy, committing to finding proper sources if inspired by ChatGPT rather than direct use.

### Study Focus
The empirical findings of LLMs like ChatGPT within higher education were investigated using the UTAUT2 theoretical framework focusing on human-computer interaction in information systems. The research examined the impact of LLMs on traditional academic dynamics regarding ethical, behavioral, social, and educational implications.

### Key Themes Identified
- **Academic Integrity and Policy Regulation**
- **Accessibility and Inclusion for Diverse Students**
- **Teaching Methodologies and Curriculum Integration**
- **Student-Teacher Interpersonal Dynamics**
- **Ethical Considerations Requiring Guidelines**
- **Human Component Concerns Regarding Reduced Interaction**
- **Data Privacy, Consent, and Transparency Issues**

### Academic Integrity Concerns
Risks include academic dishonesty (essay writing without participation), plagiarism via AI-generated content based on parameters rather than original work, misuse undermining research integrity, and potential for students to complete tasks without connecting with subject matter due to reliance on quick responses.

### Bias & Accuracy Issues
LLMs are susceptible to biases and errors in training material as they rely on patterns rather than facts; rapid generation of thorough responses can lead to a lack of critical engagement or memorization-based assessments.

### Ethical Challenges
Encompasses data privacy, consent regarding student data handling, transparency in data collection, algorithmic bias from processing vast personal data, and the need for strict protection guidelines by educational institutions.

### Accessibility & Equity Issues
Students lack free access to advanced LLM versions leading to disparities; geographical factors create divides between technologically connected areas and rural/underserved locations; unequal access risks worsening existing inequities due to the digital divide.

### Pedagogical Impacts
Challenges include hindering critical thinking skills through increased AI reliance, potential obsolescence of traditional learning methods for tasks ranging from cooking to coding, and shifts in teacher roles from information delivery to mentoring/facilitation requiring continuous professional development.

### Teaching & Assessment Strategies
Recommendations involve customizing assessments, leveraging LLMs as teaching resources (e.g., creating outlines/quizzes), minimizing memorization demands, promoting critical engagement curricula, using AI patterns for assessment adaptation, and faculty training on identifying fraud strategies.

### Institutional Responsibilities
Institutions should supply all required AI technologies to alleviate economic inequality, create policies defining appropriate use in research/education, establish precise standards ensuring fair access, develop training initiatives, support balanced integration, regularly evaluate impact, guarantee security of sensitive student information, and define responsible usage guidelines.

### Future Directions & Research Gaps
Future studies are needed on long-term effects on learning outcomes, dynamic between students and teachers (assistance positions/communication styles), assessment methods reflecting AI-integrated curricula, establishing ethical frameworks for data privacy/integrity, understanding modifications to teacher responsibilities supporting critical thinking, anticipating legislative impacts, and investigating the quick development of LLMs.

### Benefits & Acceptance Factors
Participants view LLMs as beneficial due to better accessibility/efficiency (Performance Expectancy), ease of use reducing effort (Effort Expectancy), peer/instructor endorsements influencing attitudes (Social Influence), infrastructure support like high-speed internet being crucial (Facilitating Conditions), enjoyment linked to novelty/interactivity (Hedonic Motivation).

### Cost Considerations
Price value remains relevant due to concerns about future costs for premium features despite institutions often covering expenses.

### Policy Recommendations
A complete ban is not advised; instead, educators should use LLMs as resources while avoiding total reliance; comprehensive strategies are currently in the developmental phase amidst visible risks.

---

**Q:** summarize: journal6.pdf

**A:** **Document Type:** Academic/Technical  
**Structure:** Purpose, Methodology, Key Findings, Conclusions  

---

### **Purpose**  
The study investigates the impact of Large Language Models (LLMs), particularly ChatGPT, on university students and instructors, focusing on ethical, behavioral, and social ramifications in higher education settings. It examines how LLMs influence traditional educational dynamics and explores opportunities and challenges in their integration into academic environments. The research also addresses concerns related to academic integrity, accessibility, and the ethical use of AI technologies in education.

---

### **Methodology**  
The research employs a **qualitative methodology** with a **phenomenological approach**, using **online surveys** and **semi-structured interviews** as primary data collection methods. The **Unified Theory of Acceptance and Use of Technology 2 (UTAUT2)** serves as the theoretical framework, incorporating constructs such as Performance Expectancy, Effort Expectancy, Social Influence, Facilitating Conditions, Hedonic Motivation, Price Value, and Habit.  

Data collection involved **eight interviews** with a mix of teachers and students, and **15 online survey participants** (7 students and 8 faculty members). Surveys were conducted via **Google Forms**, consisting of 19 questions (7 open-ended, 12 closed-ended), and data collection ran from **April 24 to May 3, 2024**. **Thematic analysis** was used for data analysis, and **validity and reliability** were ensured through transparency, consistency, and adherence to ethical standards, including **informed consent, confidentiality, and anonymity**.  

The **constructivist paradigm** was chosen, emphasizing the understanding of context and meanings through qualitative methods.  

---

### **Key Findings**  
- **Academic Integrity:** Participants expressed concerns about **academic dishonesty, plagiarism, and the need for monitoring and controlling LLM usage**. A majority of survey participants believed that LLMs could **slightly increase academic integrity issues**.  
- **Ethical Concerns:** Key ethical issues include **data privacy, algorithmic bias, accountability, transparency, and effects on student agency and autonomy**. There are also concerns about **perpetuating prejudices, reinforcing inequalities, and reducing critical thinking skills** if students rely too heavily on LLMs.  
- **Accessibility and Inclusion:** **Paywalls and limited free trials** in LLMs were identified as barriers to **equal access**, potentially **exacerbating educational inequities**.  
- **Teaching and Learning Dynamics:** LLMs like ChatGPT have the potential to **enhance critical thinking, personalize learning, and improve course design**. However, they may also **reduce teacher-student interaction** and **alter traditional teaching methods**.  
- **UTAUT2 Constructs:** Most participants viewed LLMs as **beneficial for learning experiences** (Performance Expectancy), **easy to use** (Effort Expectancy), and **influenced by social factors** (Social Influence). **Hedonic Motivation** was high due to the **enjoyment of using novel, interactive tools**.  
- **Future Perspectives:** The integration of LLMs is expected to **reshape academic environments**, necessitating **ethical guidelines, training, and stakeholder engagement** for responsible implementation.  

---

### **Conclusions**  
The study highlights both the **potential benefits and significant hurdles** of integrating LLMs like ChatGPT into university education. While LLMs offer opportunities for **personalized learning, research support, and interactive teaching**, they also raise **ethical, social, and academic integrity concerns**. The findings emphasize the need for **clear guidelines, institutional support, and training** to ensure responsible and equitable use of AI in education. Future research should focus on **long-term impacts on learning outcomes, ethical implementation, and strategies for mitigating risks** associated with AI integration in higher education.  

---

**Contributors:**  
- **Sarita Namuduri** contributed to the **thesis title selection, literature review, theoretical framework development, methodological approach, survey analysis, and contact efforts**.  
- **Tadiwos Gebre Hiwot** refined the **thesis title and research question**, and contributed to the **introduction, abstract, acknowledgements, data collection and analysis methods, empirical findings, discussion, and ethical considerations**.  
- Both authors **collaboratively conducted interviews, analyzed data, and integrated research components** into a unified document.  

--- 

**Limitations:**  
- The study may have **limited generalizability** due to the focus on **Swedish universities and other geographical locations**, excluding groups such as **high school students and research institutions**.  
- **Methodological challenges** and **knowledge gaps** remain, requiring further exploration in future research.

**A:** ### Purpose

The study aims to investigate the influence of Large Language Models (LLMs) like ChatGPT on higher education, focusing on technology acceptance through a qualitative research approach.

### Methodology

- **Adopted Methodology**: The study employs a qualitative research methodology paired with a phenomenological approach. Data is gathered through online surveys and semi-structured interviews with university students and teachers using the Unified Theory of Acceptance and Use of Technology 2 (UTAUT2) as its theoretical framework.
  
- **Literature Review / Cited Work**:
  - UTAUT identifies seven indicators: Performance Expectancy, Effort Expectancy, Social Influence, Facilitating Conditions, Hedonic Motivation, Price Value, and Habit.
  - Venkatesh et al. (2012) found that performance expectancy predicts behavioural intention, consistent with the original model.
  - Meyliana et al. (2019) define Effort Expectancy regarding the degree of work required to run a particular technology and its ease of use perception.

### Key Findings by Theme

- **Technology Acceptance Factors**: The study identified factors such as performance expectancy, effort expectancy, social influence, facilitating conditions, hedonic motivation, price value, and habit influencing the acceptance of LLMs in educational settings.

  - Participants reported minimal personal use due to plagiarism concerns but acknowledged potential utility in engineering fields (Participant H).

  - Preference for free versions over university-provided advanced tools (Participant B).
  - Utilization for research and summarizing literature (Participant C).
  - Concerns about academic dishonesty if unmonitored, suggesting ethical guidelines (Participants A, E).

- **Ethical Considerations**:
  - Skepticism about AI detection technologies and the difficulty in validating student effort (Participants C, G).
  - Need for explicit guidelines governing LLM usage by both students and teachers to prevent undermining academic integrity (Participants B, D, E).
  - Ethical considerations apply equally across professional levels (Participant D).

- **Access and Inequality**:
  - Paywalls limiting access to accurate models for those without financial means (Participant A).
  - Unequal access due to paid services leading to inequity among students with varying financial capacities (Participants G, E).
  - Geographical and infrastructure disparities creating access gaps regardless of financial capability (Participant F).

- **Impact on Teacher-Student Dynamics**:
  - Introduction of tools changes teacher-student dynamics from a dyad to a triad including the tool (Participant B).
  - Potential decline in teacher-student relationships as teachers struggle to verify student work and traditional methods face skepticism from newer generations of educators (Participants C, G).

### Ethical Implications

- The study emphasizes the critical need for uniform policies and rigorous ethical oversight to integrate AI tools effectively in educational settings.

### Conclusion

The research findings highlight a complex interplay between technology acceptance and ethical considerations. Participants' experiences underscore the importance of clear guidelines, ethical practices, and addressing issues related to access and inequality when integrating LLMs into higher education.

**A:** # Summary of Research on the Impact of Large Language Models (LLMs) Like ChatGPT in Higher Education

## Purpose
The primary goal of this thesis is to investigate the impact of LLMs like ChatGPT on university students and instructors, focusing on usage patterns, academic performance, information retention, evaluation, critical thinking, and ethical implications. The study explores how these AI language models influence traditional higher education dynamics from ethical, behavioural, and social standpoints.

## Methodology
- **[Adopted Methodology]** The study examines the use of LLMs in higher education settings, focusing on ethical, behavioural, and social challenges.
- **[Adopted Methodology]** Data collection methods include interviews and online surveys conducted primarily in Swedish universities and other geographical locations.
- **[Adopted Methodology]** The UTAUT2 framework is used to explore how LLMs are integrated into educational contexts, focusing on constructs such as performance expectancy, effort expectancy, social influence, facilitating conditions, hedonic motivation, price value, and habit.
- **[Adopted Methodology]** Thematic analysis was used to interpret findings from both interviews and surveys.
- **[Adopted Methodology]** Ethical considerations included informed consent, confidentiality, and voluntary participation.

## Key Findings by Theme

### Usage and Perceptions
- **Participant A**: Uses LLMs for creating educational content after verifying accuracy. Emphasizes the risk of academic dishonesty if not monitored and suggests institutional-level ethical guidelines.
- **Participant B**: Prefers the free version of ChatGPT over university-provided Copilot, finds it more useful. Proposes explicit guidelines for using LLMs, distinguishing between brainstorming and content creation.
- **Participant C**: Sees value in research and time-saving with ChatGPT and Copilot. Expresses concerns about reduced teacher-student interaction and intellectual property issues.
- **Participant D**: Highlights different ethical considerations for teachers versus students. Argues that even the free version of ChatGPT is accessible to all backgrounds, emphasizing skill development for optimal use.
- **Participant E**: Views AI tools as having a dual nature—both a risk for academic dishonesty and a potential tool for detecting it. Notes the evolving skills required from students due to LLMs.
- **Participant F**: Prioritizes academic integrity, ensuring correct citation of sources even when inspired by LLMs.
- **Participant G**: Points out inequality due to financial barriers in accessing better AI services. Discusses potential negative impacts of LLMs, such as reduced critical thinking and dependency.

### Ethical and Social Implications
- **Participant H**: Uses ChatGPT occasionally but avoids educational purposes due to plagiarism concerns. Notes that ethical concerns like plagiarism have always existed and are not fully preventable.
- **Survey Responses**: Highlight the need for clear guidelines on integrating LLMs into education. Concerns over academic integrity and the potential for LLMs to promote inclusivity and accessibility.
- **Survey Responses**: Indicate that LLMs can facilitate collaborative learning but may reduce classroom interaction between students and teachers.
- **Participants**: Express concerns about data privacy, algorithmic bias, and the digital divide caused by LLMs. Note that access to AI technologies can exacerbate educational inequities if not distributed fairly.

### Educational Impact
- **Participants**: Believe that LLMs can enhance learning experiences through personalized learning, enriched content, and research support. However, they caution that over-reliance on LLMs may reduce critical thinking and teacher-student interaction.
- **Participant A**: Urges maintaining student critical thinking while using LLMs creatively. Emphasizes the need for innovative pedagogical strategies when integrating LLMs into teaching.
- **Participant E**: Notes that teachers can benefit from using LLMs to design assignments and lectures but warns of ethical issues.
- **Participant C**: Observes current skepticism among traditional teachers towards AI integration, affecting teacher-student relationships.

## Ethical Implications
- Ethical considerations include academic integrity, data privacy, algorithmic bias, and the digital divide. Proactive measures such as monitoring usage, setting limits, and developing ethical guidelines are essential.
- Collaboration between educators, researchers, policymakers, and ethicists is crucial for addressing these issues. Institutions should create policies defining the appropriate use of AI tools in academic research.
- Educating teachers and students on the ethical usage of LLMs is crucial for their responsible adoption and implementation.

## Conclusion
The study reveals a complex interplay of factors influencing the adoption and impact of LLMs in higher education. While LLMs offer benefits such as personalized learning, enriched content, and research support, they also raise ethical and social challenges. The findings can guide decisions on the adoption and use of LLMs, helping educators, instructional designers, and policymakers understand both the advantages and drawbacks. Future research should focus on establishing ethical guidelines and understanding the long-term impacts of LLMs on traditional educational roles and communication styles.

## Purpose  
This study investigates the impact of Large Language Models (LLMs), particularly ChatGPT, on university students and instructors. It explores usage patterns, perspectives, academic performance, information retention, evaluation, critical thinking, and ethical implications of integrating LLMs into higher education.

## Methodology  
- **Adopted Methodology**: A qualitative research approach was employed, combining **semi-structured interviews** and **online surveys**.

- Participants expressed significant concerns about **academic dishonesty**, such as using LLMs to generate essays or complete assignments, potentially leading to **plagiarism**.

- There is a need for **policies and ethical guidelines** to ensure responsible use of LLMs in education and to safeguard **academic integrity**.

### 2. **Accessibility and Inclusion**  
- While LLMs can enhance **accessibility** to educational resources, there is a risk that they may **exacerbate existing disparities**.  
- Students with **learning challenges** may be disadvantaged if they lack **equal access** to advanced AI tools.  
- **Fair access** to AI technologies is crucial to prevent the **digital divide** from widening.

- LLMs can be used by teachers to **create course materials, quizzes, coding, grading, and scientific publications**, enhancing **pedagogical efficiency**.  
- However, there is a risk that students may become overly **dependent on technology**, potentially **hindering critical thinking** and **problem-solving skills**.  
- LLMs have the potential to **personalize learning experiences** by adapting course materials to individual student needs.

### 4. **Student-Teacher Relationship and Interpersonal Dynamics**  
- The use of LLMs may **reduce in-person interaction** between students and teachers, potentially **diminishing the human component** of education.  
- There is a need for educators to **adapt their roles**, moving towards **mentoring and facilitation** in an AI-enhanced learning environment.

### 5. **Technology Acceptance and Use**  
- Participants generally found LLMs **easy to use**, which positively influenced their **acceptance and continued use**, supporting the **effort expectancy** component of UTAUT2.  
- **Access to high-speed internet** and **training on LLMs** were crucial for adoption, aligning with the **facilitating conditions** construct of UTAUT2.  
- **Frequent use** of LLMs led to **habitual use**, especially when seamlessly integrated into **learning management systems**.

### 6. **Future Perspectives and Recommendations**  
- The study highlights the **transformational potential** of LLMs in higher education, including **efficiency, accessibility, and customization**.  
- **Ethical concerns** such as **data privacy, academic integrity, and algorithmic bias** require attention.  
- The research recommends **establishing standards for AI use**, ensuring **fair access**, developing **training initiatives**, and supporting a **balanced approach** to AI integration.  
- **Future research** should focus on **ethical guidelines**, the **impact of LLMs on educational methodologies**, **long-term impacts on student-teacher dynamics**, and **assessment methods** in AI-integrated learning environments.

## Ethical Implications  
- The study emphasizes the **need for ethical oversight** and **rigorous policies** to ensure responsible AI integration in education.  
- **Data confidentiality** and **bias mitigation** are essential to ensure **equitable and ethical use** of LLMs in academic contexts.

## Conclusion  
This study provides a foundational discussion on the **dual potential** of LLMs to **revolutionize and complicate educational processes**. It underscores the importance of **balanced strategies** that prioritize **innovation and ethical responsibility**. - **Academic Dishonesty**: Participants expressed significant concerns about the **risks of academic dishonesty** and the **unethical use of LLMs**, emphasizing the need for **policies and ethical guidelines** to ensure responsible usage.
- **Plagiarism and Reliability**: There are fears that students might use these tools for **cheating** by submitting **non-original work**, which could **undermine the purpose of higher education**. Additionally, participants raised concerns about **receiving false information** from LLMs due to **biases and errors** in training data.
- **Ethical Issues**: **Data privacy**, **consent**, and **algorithmic bias** are significant concerns when integrating LLMs into educational contexts. **Strict guidelines** must be followed to **protect student data** and ensure **fairness**.

- **Benefits for Teachers**: Teachers can use LLMs to **create instructional materials**, **save time**, and **improve course quality** through the **synthesis of online resources**.
- **Impact on Critical Thinking**: There is a concern that **over-reliance on LLMs** might **reduce critical thinking skills** among students. Some participants emphasized the need to **use LLMs creatively as complementary tools** rather than **replacements** for traditional teaching methods.

- **Inclusivity and Accessibility**: LLMs have the potential to **increase equitable access** to educational resources through features like **text-to-speech** and **translation**. However, **unequal access** to these tools could **exacerbate existing disparities** in educational opportunities.

- **Curriculum Integration**: LLMs can be used to **enhance various aspects of the educational experience**, including **personalized learning** and **research support**. However, there is a need to **balance the use of LLMs** with traditional teaching methods to **maintain critical thinking skills**.
- **Institutional Support**: **Infrastructure and support** provided by institutions are crucial for the **adoption of LLMs**. Participants noted that **peer and instructor endorsements** significantly influenced their **attitudes and likelihood of using LLMs**.
- **Teacher Role Evolution**: Educators are expected to take on more **advanced roles**, such as **mentoring and facilitation**, in an **AI-enhanced learning environment**.

### Future Perspectives
- **Ethical and Legal Considerations**: The **development and application of LLMs** must adhere to **ethical and legal considerations**, including **fraud**, **privacy**, **discrimination**, and **other undesired reasons**.
- **Collaboration and Regulation**: **Collaboration between humans and AI** is a promising strategy for using LLMs in education. The study recommends **establishing precise standards** for the use of AI, **ensuring fair access to resources**, **developing training initiatives**, and **supporting balanced technological integration**.

## Ethical Implications
- **Data Privacy and Consent**: Ethical guidelines must be followed to **protect student data** and ensure **informed consent**.
- **Bias and Fairness**: The study highlights the need to **address algorithmic bias** and **ensure fairness** in the use of LLMs.
- **Academic Integrity**: The **risks of academic dishonesty** and the **need for policies and ethical guidelines** are emphasized to ensure **responsible usage** of LLMs in educational contexts.

  - Suggests gradual transformation of traditional teaching methods to introduce technology responsibly.

- **Participant D**:
  - Emphasizes the need for educators to declare their use of AI tools to editors and journal editorial members.
  - Identifies gaps in guidelines for using AI at the pedagogical level, noting how to benefit from it remains an "open box" requiring investigation by policymakers alongside educators.

- **Participant E**:
  - Describes AI as a "two-sided weapon," highlighting risks of student use versus opportunities to detect text generated during submissions.
  - Advocates for designing examination procedures that balance inequalities among students using learning analytics and AI.

- **Participant F**: 
  - Notes the significant disparity in accessibility based on geography, with established nations having better infrastructure compared to developing regions.

- **Participant G**:
  - Stresses the creation of inequality due to limited access to premium services; highlights the need for equitable distribution of resources.

### Ethical Implications

- **Academic Integrity**: 
  - Concerns about LLMs encouraging dishonesty by allowing task completion without active participation.
  - Risks of false information and biases in AI responses.
- **Data Privacy & Security**:
  - Importance of strict adherence to data protection guidelines due to the large amounts of sensitive student data collected by LLMs.

### Conclusion

The study highlights the dual nature of LLMs, presenting both opportunities and challenges for higher education. While participants generally view these tools positively, they emphasize the need for ethical regulation, transparent policies, and balanced integration into teaching methods to ensure academic integrity and equitable access.

**A:** # Summary of Research on the Impact of LLMs Like ChatGPT in Higher Education

## Purpose
This study investigates the impact of Large Language Models (LLMs) such as ChatGPT on university students and instructors, focusing on usage patterns, perspectives, academic performance, information retention, evaluation, critical thinking, and ethical implications.

## Methodology
- **Adopted Methodology**: The study employs a **qualitative research approach** grounded in **phenomenology** and guided by the **UTAUT2 framework**. Data was collected through **semi-structured interviews** and **online surveys** using Google Forms, with 8 interviews and 15 surveys conducted from April 24 to May 3, 2024.
- **Framework**: The **Human-Centered AI** framework and **UTAUT2 model** are used to organize and evaluate data, focusing on constructs such as **performance expectancy, effort expectancy, social influence, facilitating conditions, hedonic motivation, price value, and habit**.
- **Ethical Considerations**: The study adheres to ethical standards, including **informed consent, confidentiality, and voluntary participation**. Data was securely stored and used only for research purposes.

## Key Findings by Theme

### Academic Integrity
- **Concerns**: Participants expressed concerns about **academic dishonesty and plagiarism**, emphasizing the need for **clear guidelines and policies** for the ethical use of LLMs.
- **Solutions**: Proposals include **monitoring and regulation** by universities, **transparent declaration of AI use**, and **innovative pedagogical strategies** to ensure academic integrity.

### Accessibility and Inclusion
- **Digital Divide**: There is a **concern about the economic and geographical barriers** to accessing premium LLM features, which may **exacerbate educational inequities**.
- **Equity**: Participants highlighted the **need for fair access to AI technologies** to avoid **disparities in learning opportunities**.

### Teaching Methodologies
- **Benefits**: LLMs were seen as **valuable tools for enhancing learning**, such as **assignment creation, curriculum enrichment, and personalized learning**.
- **Drawbacks**: There is a **potential reduction in critical thinking skills** and **over-reliance on AI**, which may **diminish traditional teaching methods**.

### Student-Teacher Relationship
- **Impact on Interaction**: Some participants noted that the use of LLMs could **reduce personal interactions** between students and teachers, as students may rely more on AI for answers.
- **Need for Communication**: Clear **communication and guidelines on LLM use** are emphasized to maintain **effective teaching and learning dynamics**.

### Ethical Considerations
- **Concerns**: Ethical issues such as **data privacy, algorithmic bias, and academic integrity** are central to the use of LLMs in education.
- **Solutions**: The study recommends the **development of ethical guidelines**, **monitoring of AI use**, and **training for educators and students** on responsible AI integration.

## Ethical Implications
- The study underscores the **importance of ethical frameworks** in the use of LLMs in education, highlighting the **need for transparency, fairness, and accountability**.
- **Confidentiality** and **informed consent** are prioritized, ensuring that **participant data is protected** and used only for research purposes.

## Conclusion
- **Transformational Potential**: LLMs like ChatGPT have **significant potential to enhance learning and teaching**, offering **personalized interactions and real-time support**.
- **Challenges**: However, they also pose **ethical and pedagogical challenges**, including **academic dishonesty, reduced critical thinking, and educational inequities**.
- **Future Directions**: The study recommends **further research** on the **long-term effects of AI on learning outcomes**, **ethical frameworks**, and **pedagogical strategies** for integrating LLMs into education.

## Contributions
- This study contributes to the **understanding of user experiences with LLMs**, highlighting **preferences, challenges, and ethical concerns**.
- Findings can **inform educators, instructional designers, and policymakers** on the **responsible adoption of AI technologies** in higher education.

## [summarize] 2026-08-14 07:13

**Q:** summarize: journal6.pdf

**A:** ### Purpose

The study aims to evaluate the influence of Large Language Models (LLMs) such as ChatGPT on higher education through a qualitative research methodology paired with a phenomenological approach. The research questions focus on understanding factors influencing technology acceptance in higher education and identifying ethical considerations.

### Methodology

#### Adopted Methodology
- **Qualitative Research**: Employed online surveys and semi-structured interviews to gather data from university students and teachers.
- **Theoretical Framework**: Utilized the Unified Theory of Acceptance and Use of Technology 2 (UTAUT2) as a guiding framework for evaluating technology acceptance in higher education.

#### Literature Review
- Khlaif et al. (2023): Highlighted gaps in standardized procedures and recommended practices for using ChatGPT in scientific research.
- Venkatesh et al., 2012: Identified seven indicators for UTAUT usage, including Performance Expectancy, Effort Expectancy, Social Influence, Facilitating Conditions, Hedonic Motivation, Price Value, and Habit.
- Strzelecki (2023): Defined specific constructs of UTAUT2, including four components each for Performance Expectancy and Effort Expectancy, three components for Social Influence, four components for Facilitating Conditions, and "Behavioral intention" with three components.

### Key Findings by Theme

#### Participant Insights
- **Personal Usage and Perceptions**:
  - **Participant H**: Varies in LLM usage intensity; avoids educational use due to plagiarism concerns but uses it for engineering solutions when asked. Prefers free versions over university-provided Copilot.
  - **Participant A**: Uses LLMs (ChatGPT or Co-pilot) for creating questions and problems, double-checking results, and ensuring thorough review of outcomes. Recognizes potential bias in tools.
  - **Participant B**: Uses only the free version of ChatGPT despite access to a university-provided Copilot; finds free versions more useful due to specific work requirements.
  - **Participant C**: Prefers using free LLMs like ChatGPT and Claude, values their capacity for summarizing literature. Skeptical about technologies detecting AI-generated content, believes current methods are impractical.

- **Ethical Considerations**:
  - **Participant A**: Conducted a prior study with bachelor students from the Department of Informatics; identified academic integrity as a theme where respondents admitted ChatGPT can create dishonesty if not properly monitored.
  - **Participant B**: Emphasizes the need for explicit standards governing how both students and teachers should use technology to ensure integrity, highlighting the seriousness of these rules.
  - **Participant D**: Notes that ethical considerations differ between roles; as a teacher, they primarily use it for research assistance where ethics may seem less sensitive compared to student submissions requiring strict adherence.
  - **Participant E**: Views LLMs as a "two-sided weapon" with potential to both undermine and maintain academic integrity depending on application context.

- **Inequity Issues**:
  - **Participant A & G, Participant C, Participant F**: Highlighted financial barriers where premium features are not accessible due to cost. Emphasized the need for equitable access regardless of financial capability.
  - **Participant E**: Observed that most AI tools offer limited functionality unless paid for full access, creating significant barriers especially among students lacking funds.

- **Impact on Teaching and Learning**:
  - **Participant D**: Argued against concerns about accessibility limitations within generative AI because free versions like ChatGPT 3.5 allow benefits to be realized by students regardless of background.
  - **Participant A & C**: Emphasized the need for innovative pedagogical strategies acceptable to students while maintaining quality and meeting learning objectives when integrating LLMs into curricula.

- **Ethical Concerns**:
  - **Participant G**: Noted negative impacts where people stop using their minds or brains because answers come instantly via clicks, leading to a lack of effort input and critical thinking deficiency.
  - **Participant E**: Highlighted advantages for teachers including the ability to design assignments and structure lectures using synthesized experiences from existing online courses.

### Ethical Implications

- Ensured clear guidelines, policies, ethical review boards, transparency, accountability measures regarding bias and fairness, privacy protection, data security safeguards, and stakeholder engagement are necessary.
- Addressed potential negative impacts on teacher-student interactions and the risk of overreliance on AI technologies leading to reduced critical thinking.

### Conclusion

The study found that LLMs such as ChatGPT have both positive and negative implications for higher education. While they offer significant benefits in terms of information accessibility and efficiency, ethical considerations and equitable access remain crucial issues. The findings suggest the need for well-defined policies, guidelines, and innovative pedagogical strategies to integrate these tools effectively into academic settings.

## [summarize] 2026-08-14 07:33

**Q:** summarize: journal6.pdf

**A:** # Summary of Research on the Ethical, Behavioural, and Social Ramifications of Deploying LLMs in University Settings

## Purpose
This study critically examines the ethical, behavioural, and social ramifications of deploying Large Language Models (LLMs) like ChatGPT in university settings. It explores the dual potential of LLMs to revolutionize and complicate educational processes, emphasizing the need for balanced strategies that prioritize innovation and ethical responsibility.

## Methodology
The study employs a **qualitative research methodology** paired with a **phenomenological approach** to gather data through **online surveys** and **semi-structured interviews** with university students and teachers. The theoretical framework guiding this investigation is the **Unified Theory of Acceptance and Use of Technology 2 (UTAUT2)**.

## Key Findings by Theme

### Technology Acceptance and UTAUT2 Constructs
- **Performance Expectancy**: Participants viewed LLMs like ChatGPT as highly beneficial for enhancing learning experiences, with improved accessibility to information and increased efficiency in academic tasks.
- **Effort Expectancy**: LLMs were generally found to be easy to use, reducing the effort required for learning and administrative tasks.
- **Social Influence**: Peer and instructor endorsements significantly influenced participants' attitudes and likelihood of using LLMs.
- **Facilitating Conditions**: Institutional support, such as access to high-speed internet and training on LLMs, was crucial for adoption.
- **Hedonic Motivation**: Many users expressed enjoyment and satisfaction in using LLMs, especially among students who found traditional learning methods less engaging.
- **Price Value**: While less prominent, concerns about future costs associated with premium features were noted.
- **Habit**: Frequent use of LLMs led to habitual use, particularly when integrated into learning management systems.

### Ethical Considerations
- **Academic Integrity**: Participants expressed concerns about plagiarism, critical thinking, and the need for clear guidelines to ensure that LLMs enhance rather than replace human instruction.
- **Data Privacy and Algorithmic Bias**: Issues such as data privacy, algorithmic bias, and accountability were raised as significant ethical concerns.
- **Intellectual Property**: Concerns were raised about content used without credit and compensation for creators.
- **Critical Thinking and Dependency**: Some participants highlighted the risk of diminishing critical thinking and over-reliance on AI.

### Educational Implications
- **Impact on Teaching and Learning**: LLMs were seen as tools that could save time and enhance teaching efficiency, but also posed challenges to traditional teaching methods and student engagement.
- **Need for Guidelines and Policies**: Participants emphasized the need for ethical guidelines, training, and institutional policies to manage the integration of LLMs in education.
- **Adaptation and Future Use**: There was a consensus that LLMs are inevitable in education, requiring proactive adaptation by academia and the development of multidisciplinary strategies involving educators, researchers, policymakers, and ethicists.

## Ethical Implications
The study underscores the critical need for **uniform policies** and **rigorous ethical oversight** to integrate AI tools effectively in educational settings. It highlights the importance of **transparency, accountability, and fairness** in the use of LLMs, as well as the need to **protect privacy and data security**. Ethical review boards and stakeholder engagement were recommended as essential components of responsible AI adoption in education.

## Conclusion
This study provides insights into the evolving role of technology in defining the twenty-first-century educational landscape. It highlights the **dual potential** of LLMs to both enhance and complicate educational processes. The research emphasizes the need for a **balanced approach** that promotes innovation while upholding ethical principles, ensuring that the integration of LLMs in education supports the development of critical thinking, academic integrity, and equitable learning outcomes.

**A:** # Final Summary  

## Purpose  
This study investigates the impact of Large Language Models (LLMs) like ChatGPT on university students and instructors, focusing on ethical, behavioral, and social challenges in higher education. It aims to explore the evolving role of AI in education, evaluating benefits, problems, and future opportunities.  

## Methodology  
- **Adopted Methodology**: Qualitative approach with **phenomenological methodology**, using **online surveys** and **semi-structured interviews** as data collection methods.  
- **Adopted Methodology**: Data collection conducted in **Swedish universities** and **geographically diverse institutions**.  
- **Adopted Methodology**: Analysis of **open-ended survey responses** to capture rich, descriptive insights.  
- **Adopted Methodology**: Ethical considerations addressed, ensuring compliance with standards.  

## Key Findings by Theme  

### **Academic Integrity and Ethical Concerns**  
- **57.1% of participants** reported rising concerns about academic integrity due to LLMs, with **Participant A** emphasizing risks of plagiarism and academic dishonesty if not monitored.  
- **Participant C** raised issues about **intellectual property** and compensation for content used by LLMs.  
- **Participant H** noted that historical academic dishonesty is not fully preventable by ChatGPT.  

### **Usage Patterns and Institutional Support**  
- **38.5% of participants** use LLMs extensively, while **30.8% use them occasionally**.  
- **Participant B** prefers the free version of ChatGPT over institutional tools like CoPilot, citing greater utility.  
- **Participant A** highlights the **digital divide** caused by LLMs, noting inequality in access to accurate information due to business models.  
- **Participant G** raises concerns about **financial barriers** limiting access to AI-generated content.  

### **Impact on Teaching and Learning**  
- **Participant C** sees LLMs as beneficial for **research efficiency** and **personalized learning**, but fears reduced teacher-student interaction.  
- **Participant E** discusses the **dual nature** of AI tools—both a challenge and a resource for maintaining academic integrity.  
- **Participant A** suggests **pedagogical strategies** to integrate LLMs while preserving critical thinking and meeting learning objectives.  

### **Social Influence and Institutional Infrastructure**  
- **Social influence** (peer and institutional opinions) significantly affects LLM adoption, as noted by **Participant A** and **Participant B**.  
- **Participant D** stresses the need for **transparent AI use** and **ethical guidelines** to address dependency and ensure fairness.  
- **Participant E** emphasizes the role of **learning analytics** in designing assessments to monitor student progress.  

## Ethical Implications  
- **Data privacy**, **algorithmic bias**, and **academic integrity** are critical ethical challenges.  
- **Participants** recommend **developing ethical guidelines**, **training initiatives**, **transparency**, and **stakeholder engagement** to manage AI tools.  
- **Inequities** in access to AI technologies risk exacerbating existing educational disparities, necessitating **fair distribution** and **policy development**.  

## Conclusion  
LLMs like ChatGPT offer significant benefits for education, including **personalized learning**, **efficiency gains**, and **enhanced engagement**. However, their integration raises pressing ethical, social, and pedagogical challenges. The study recommends **establishing precise AI usage standards**, **ensuring equitable access**, and **fostering institutional support** to maximize benefits while mitigating risks. Future research should focus on **policy frameworks**, **teacher training**, and **long-term impacts** on educational equity and critical thinking.

## [summarize] 2026-08-14 08:46

**Q:** summarize: journal6.pdf

**A:** # Summary of Research on the Ethical, Behavioural, and Social Ramifications of Deploying LLMs in University Settings

## Purpose  
This study critically examines the ethical, behavioural, and social ramifications of deploying Large Language Models (LLMs) such as ChatGPT in university settings. It explores how these technologies influence learning outcomes, academic integrity, teacher-student dynamics, and institutional policies.

## Methodology  
The research employs a **qualitative research methodology** paired with a **phenomenological approach**, gathering data through **online surveys and semi-structured interviews** with university students and teachers. The study is guided by the **Unified Theory of Acceptance and Use of Technology 2 (UTAUT2)**, which provides a theoretical framework to assess technology acceptance and use. The **constructivist paradigm** underpins the research design, emphasizing the subjective experiences of participants.

Data collection took place in **Swedish universities and universities from different geographical locations**, with a total of **15 respondents** (7 students and 8 faculty members). Surveys were conducted using **Google Forms** from **April 24 to May 3, 2024**, and interviews were conducted between **March 22 and May 2, 2024**. **Thematic analysis** was used to interpret qualitative data, ensuring **reliability and validity** through transparency, consistency, and informed consent.

## Key Findings by Theme  

### 1. **Technology Acceptance and Use (UTAUT2 Constructs)**  
- **Performance Expectancy (PE):** Most participants viewed LLMs as beneficial for enhancing learning experiences, particularly in improving accessibility to information and increasing efficiency in academic tasks.  
- **Effort Expectancy (EE):** LLMs were found to be easy to use, significantly reducing the effort required for learning and administrative tasks.  
- **Social Influence (SI):** Participants noted that peers' and instructors' opinions influenced their attitudes and likelihood of using LLMs.  
- **Facilitating Conditions (FC):** Institutional support, such as access to high-speed internet and training on LLMs, was crucial for adoption.  
- **Hedonic Motivation (HM):** Many users expressed enjoyment and satisfaction from using LLMs, attributed to their novel and interactive nature.  
- **Price Value (PV):** While less prominent, concerns about future costs of premium features were noted.  
- **Habit (HT):** Frequent use of LLMs led to habitual use, especially when integrated into learning management systems.  

### 2. **Ethical and Social Implications**  
- **Academic Integrity:** Concerns were raised about plagiarism, academic dishonesty, and the potential for LLMs to replace critical thinking.  
- **Inequity:** Access to LLMs may exacerbate existing educational inequalities, particularly for students who lack financial resources to access premium tools.  
- **Teacher-Student Dynamics:** Some participants expressed concerns that LLMs could reduce in-class interaction and diminish the role of educators. Others believed that LLMs could enhance personalized learning and improve student-teacher relationships if used responsibly.  
- **Institutional Responsibility:** Participants emphasized the need for **ethical guidelines, training, and oversight** to ensure responsible use of LLMs.  

### 3. **Perceptions of Teachers and Students**  
- **Teachers:**  
  - **Participant A:** Used LLMs to create questions and problems but noted the need for critical review due to potential biases or errors.  
  - **Participant B:** Preferred the free version of ChatGPT over university-provided tools, finding it more useful.  
  - **Participant D:** Advocated for clear guidelines to ensure LLMs enhance, rather than replace, human instruction.  
  - **Participant E:** Highlighted the need for careful integration of LLMs into curricula to avoid negative impacts on student skills.  

- **Students:**  
  - **Participant C:** Believed LLMs could save time and enhance research but raised concerns about intellectual property and the erosion of critical thinking.  
  - **Participant F:** Emphasized the importance of academic integrity and avoided using LLMs for original work.  
  - **Participant G:** Noted that LLMs could lead to inequity if access is not fair.  
  - **Participant H:** Used LLMs sparingly, citing concerns about plagiarism and ethical use.  

## Ethical Implications  
The study underscores the need for **ethical oversight, transparency, and accountability** in the use of LLMs in education. Key ethical concerns include:  
- **Data privacy and security** (Zhai, 2022; Rane et al., 2023)  
- **Academic integrity** (Cotton et al., 2023; Dempere et al., 2023)  
- **Algorithmic bias** (Rane et al., 2023; Vargas-Murillo et al., 2023)  
- **Equity in access** (Vargas-Murillo et al., 2023)  

## Conclusion  
LLMs like ChatGPT have the potential to **enhance educational processes** by improving efficiency, personalizing learning, and supporting teaching. However, their integration must be accompanied by **rigorous ethical oversight, institutional policies, and training** to address concerns around academic integrity, equity, and the preservation of critical thinking. The study highlights the need for a **balanced approach** that leverages the benefits of AI while mitigating its risks.

## Purpose
This study provides a comprehensive review of the developments in Large Language Models (LLMs), summarizing significant findings from existing literature and offering a detailed analysis of design aspects, including architectures, datasets, and training pipelines.

## Methodology
- [Adopted Methodology] The article reviews developments in LLMs, identifying crucial architectural components and training strategies employed by different models.
- [Adopted Methodology] It discusses performance differences in zero-shot and few-shot settings, along with the impact of fine-tuning and comparisons between supervised and generalized models and encoder vs. decoder vs. encoder-decoder architectures.
- [Adopted Methodology] A comprehensive review of multi-modal LLMs, retrieval augmented LLMs, LLM-powered agents, efficient LLMs, datasets, evaluation, applications, and challenges is provided.
- [Adopted Methodology] The document references various large language models and their methodologies, including training techniques like mixed precision training, retrieval-augmented generation, and the application of these models in various domains such as code generation, financial modeling, and medical reasoning.
- [Adopted Methodology] The document mentions techniques for improving model performance through methods like instruction tuning and fine-tuning with human feedback.
- [Adopted Methodology] The document discusses the use of large language models for reasoning tasks, such as tool usage and embodied reasoning.
- [Adopted Methodology] The document highlights the integration of large language models with real-world applications via RESTful APIs.
- [Adopted Methodology] The document mentions the use of large language models for autonomous agents in medical reasoning and robotic skill synthesis.
- [Adopted Methodology] A comprehensive survey of applications, challenges, limitations, and future prospects is provided.

## Key Findings by Theme

### Architectural Innovations
- **T5** is an encoder-decoder model employing a unified text-to-text training approach for all NLP problems.
- **mT5** is a multilingual extension of T5, trained on the mC4 dataset with 101 languages.
- **GPT-3** is a large-scale model that demonstrates improved performance with increased parameter size and uses a combination of dense and sparse attention in transformer layers.
- **CPM-2** is a bilingual (English and Chinese) model that uses a mixture-of-experts (MoE) architecture and a memory-efficient framework for inference.
- **PaLM** is a causal decoder with optimizations such as SwiGLU activation and RoPE embeddings, which improve training efficiency.
- **LLaMA** is a set of decoder-only models with efficient causal attention and grouped-query attention in its variants.
- **Mixtral8x22b** is a MoE model with eight distinct experts, each token routed to two experts per layer.
- **Snowflake Arctic** is a hybrid of dense and MoE architectures, with a large number of experts to increase model capacity.
- **Grok** is a family of LLMs with a MoE architecture, including Grok-1 and Grok-1.5, the latter being a multi-modal LLM.
- **Gemini** is a multi-modal LLM with MoE architecture, achieving human-level capabilities on the MMLU benchmark.
- **Nemotron-4 340B** is a decoder-only model aligned on a large proportion of synthetic data, improving model performance significantly.

### Training Strategies
- **Transfer Learning** is widely used to fine-tune pre-trained LLMs on task-specific data.
- **Instruction-tuning** is used to enable models to respond effectively to user queries.
- **Alignment-tuning** aligns models using human feedback to ensure they operate according to human intentions and values.
- **Zero-Shot Prompting** allows models to answer queries without seeing any examples in the prompt.
- **In-context Learning** (few-shot learning) involves showing multiple input-output demonstration pairs to generate desired responses.
- **Chain-of-Thought (CoT)** is a special case of prompting where demonstrations contain reasoning information.
- **Self-Consistency** improves CoT performance by generating multiple responses and selecting the most frequent answer.
- **Tree-of-Thought (ToT)** explores multiple reasoning paths for problem-solving.
- **Data Parallelism, Tensor Parallelism, Pipeline Parallelism, and Model Parallelism** are used for distributed LLM training.
- **3D Parallelism** combines data, tensor, and model parallelism for large-scale training.
- **Optimizer Parallelism (Zero Redundancy Optimizer)** reduces memory consumption while keeping communication costs low.

### Datasets and Training
- **T5** is trained on the C4 dataset.
- **GPT-3** is trained on Common Crawl, WebText, Books Corpora, and Wikipedia.
- **mT5** is trained on the mC4 dataset.
- **PanGu-α** is trained on a 1.1TB Chinese Text Corpus.
- **CPM-2** is trained on the WuDaoCorpus dataset.
- **Codex** is trained on a subset of public Python GitHub repositories.
- **ERNIE 3.0** is trained on Chinese text corpora, Baidu Search, and domain-specific data.
- **Jurassic-1** is trained on Wikipedia, OWT, Books, C4, Pile, arXiv, and GitHub.
- **HyperCLOVA** is trained on Korean blogs, community sites, and news.
- **Yuan 1.0** is trained on Common Crawl, SogouT, Sogou News, Baidu Baike, Wikipedia, and Books.
- **Gopher** is trained on subsets of MassiveWeb, C4, News, GitHub, and Wikipedia.
- **GPT-NeoX-20B** is trained on the Pile dataset.
- **OPT** is trained on RoBERTa, Pile, and PushShift.io Reddit.
- **BLOOM** is trained on the ROOTs corpus.
- **Galactica** is trained on arXiv, PMC, Semantic Scholar, Wikipedia, StackExchange, and other scientific sources.
- **GLaM** is trained on Filtered Webpages, Social media conversations, Wikipedia, Forums, Books, and News.
- **LaMDA** is trained on public dialog data, public dialog utterances, and public web documents.
- **MT-NLG** is trained on Common Crawl, Books3, OpenWebText2, Stack Exchange, PubMed Abstracts, Wikipedia, and PG-19.
- **AlphaCode** is trained on selected GitHub repositories, CodeCon-tests, Description2Code, and CodeNet.
- **Chinchilla** is trained on MassiveWeb, MassiveText, Books, C4, News, GitHub, and Wikipedia.
- **PaLM** is trained on webpages, books, Wikipedia, news, articles, source code, and social media conversations.
- **LLaMA** is trained on CommonCrawl, C4, GitHub, Wikipedia, Books, arXiv, and StackExchange.
- **PanGu-Σ** is trained on WuDaoCorpora, CLUE, Pile, C4, and Python code.
- **BloombergGPT** is trained on inPile, Pile, C4, and Wikipedia.
- **CodeT5+** is trained on CodeSearchNet and GitHub Code.
- **StarCoder** is trained on The Stack v1.2.
- **PaLM-2** is trained on Web documents, Code, Books, Maths, and Conversations.

### Applications and Performance
- **LLaMA, LLaMA-Adapter v2, and others** have shown improvements in specific tasks such as arithmetic problems and medical knowledge integration.
- **GPT-J-6B and LLaMA** have been used in multilingual settings, demonstrating their capability to handle diverse languages.
- **MetaGPT and Metagpt** have been used to facilitate multi-agent collaborative frameworks.
- **Models like MetaGPT** have been used in medical reasoning and robotic skill synthesis.
- **RestGPT** demonstrates the potential of LLMs in practical scenarios via RESTful APIs.
- **Transparency guidance for chatGPT usage in scientific writing** is recommended by B. Aczel and E.-J. Wagenmakers.
- **S. Altmäe et al.** argue that AI can be both a friend and a foe in scientific writing.

## Ethical Implications
- **Alignment-tuning** and **fine-tuning with human feedback** are crucial for ensuring models operate according to human intentions and values.
- **Ethical considerations** in the use of LLMs for scientific writing and other domains are highlighted, emphasizing the need for transparency and responsible use.

## Conclusion
This study provides a comprehensive overview of the current state of Large Language Models, their architectures, training strategies, datasets, and applications. It highlights the advancements in model design, training efficiency, and the integration of LLMs into various domains, while also addressing the ethical implications and challenges associated with their use.

**Q:** summarize: journal2.pdf

**A:** # Summary of Research on Using GPT for Market Research

## Purpose  
The working paper, authored by Donald Ngwe, James Brand, and Ayelet Israeli, explores the potential of large language models (LLMs), specifically GPT, as tools for market research. The study investigates whether LLMs can generate realistic estimates of consumer preferences, recover meaningful preference heterogeneity across demographic groups, and whether prior human survey data can be used to fine-tune an LLM for better alignment with new surveys.

## Methodology  
The authors employed several [Adopted Methodology] approaches to simulate human survey responses using GPT:  
- **Response Generation**: GPT was queried multiple times to generate a distribution of responses rather than a single one, with the temperature parameter set to 1.0 to maximize variation.  
- **Prompt Design**: GPT was prompted as if it were a customer shopping, with prompts designed to be as similar as possible to human studies, including customer attributes such as income.  
- **Conjoint Analysis**: Both GPT-generated and human responses were used to estimate willingness-to-pay (WTP) for product attributes.  
- **Fine-Tuning**: Human survey data was used to fine-tune GPT, improving alignment with new surveys within the same product category and population.  
- **Full Factorial Design**: All possible combinations of attribute levels were generated to ensure comprehensive analysis.  
- **Demographic Inclusion**: Prompts were modified to include demographic features such as income, gender, and race to mirror human studies.  

## Key Findings by Theme  

### WTP Estimation and Accuracy  
- **Baseline GPT estimates** are frequently unreliable, with overestimates of two to threefold and incorrect signs on brand preferences and screen size in laptop studies, as well as wrong signs on new toothpaste flavors.  
- **Fine-tuning** with human survey data significantly improves alignment, including for product features not present in the fine-tuning data.  
- **GPT's implied WTP** for certain consumer goods came within 50% of estimates from a recently published conjoint study.  
- **Fine-tuning** with human data from the same product category and population substantially improves alignment, including for product features not present in the fine-tuning data.  
- **Fine-tuning** increases the accuracy of WTP for storage and flips the sign of preferences for Macbooks relative to humans.  

### Demographic Heterogeneity  
- **GPT reflects large variation** between demographic groups for measures like WTP for RAM, storage, and opt-out rates.  
- **Fine-tuning with demographic information** generates WTP estimates more similar to average human sample estimates but does not fully capture heterogeneity across different demographic groups.  
- **GPT's WTP estimates vary** across demographic groups, with income and gender influencing preferences (e.g., male users show a 15.2% higher RAM preference than female users).  

### Prompting and Response Behavior  
- **GPT is significantly more likely** to choose the first option listed when presented with multiple choices.  
- **Prompt phrasing** affects the fraction of GPT survey responses in which the outside option is chosen.  
- **Specificity in requested output** affects GPT's response style and content (e.g., requesting "amount in dollars" leads to round numbers, while "amount in dollars and cents" leads to more precise responses).  

### Brand Preferences and Market Alignment  
- **GPT's brand preferences** are consistent with market shares, such as preferring Crest toothpaste over Colgate and Dove deodorant over Speed Stick.  
- **Direct and indirect elicitation approaches** can recover GPT's WTP for products, with median and average WTP values reported for specific attributes (e.g., $1.00 median WTP for fluoride in toothpaste).  

## Ethical Implications  
- **LLMs like GPT** should be used as a supplement, not a substitute, for traditional market research methods.  
- **Fine-tuning** with human data is essential for improving alignment, but baseline results are too noisy and inconsistently signed to be acted upon directly without such data.  
- **Researchers are advised** to conduct human studies first and use LLMs with fine-tuning to extrapolate to adjacent features or prompt conditions.  

## Conclusion  
While GPT can be a useful tool for generating consumer preference estimates, its baseline results are often unreliable and inconsistent with human data. However, fine-tuning with human survey data significantly improves alignment, making GPT a valuable supplement to traditional market research methods. The approach shows promise for future expansion by research firms, provided that human data from similar contexts is available.

## [summarize] 2026-08-14 10:54

**Q:** summarize: journal5.pdf

**A:** # Summary of Findings on Large Language Model (LLM) Research Trends and Practices

## Purpose
This study evaluates over 2,000 research works on large language models (LLMs) published between 2020 and 2024, focusing on reproducibility, experimental rigor, and ethical considerations. The findings are contextualized within recent meta-reviews and aim to provide actionable recommendations for improving research practices in the field.

## Methodology
[Adopted Methodology] The study employs a systematic evaluation framework based on reproducibility checklists from premier conferences and criteria related to claims at the center of controversy. It analyzes the presence and quality of key research components such as statistical tests, reproducibility, ethics disclaimers, and the use of LLMs as evaluators.

## Key Findings by Theme

### Trends in Research Practices
- There has been a **decline in the use of ethics disclaimers** and a **rise in the use of LLMs as evaluators**.
- Claims of **LLM reasoning abilities** are increasingly made without leveraging human evaluation.
- There is a **shift towards result-driven experimentation**, influenced by media scrutiny, funding sources, and the capabilities of LLMs.
- A **notable drop in the use of ethics sections** was observed between 2022–2023 and 2023–2024.

### Reproducibility and Rigor
- **Reproducibility challenges** include inconsistent documentation, rare open-sourcing of LLMs behind APIs, and difficulties with automated metrics like BLEU.
- **Measurement is difficult** due to the effectiveness and scalability of metrics, with concerns about errors and biases being rarely reported or analyzed.
- **Claims of emergent behavior** in LLMs often lack statistical tests or error breakdowns.
- **LLMs as evaluators** are increasingly used, but these papers generally have **lower-than-average proportions of research protocols**.

### Citation and Impact
- The **presence of ethics sections, limitations, and LLM evaluators** impacts the number of citations a paper receives.
- **Statistical tests, error breakdowns, and non-English evaluations** are associated with higher citation numbers.
- **Open-sourcing, LLM evaluators, and ethics sections** are associated with lower citation numbers, suggesting that their presence does not significantly influence citation volume.

### Recommendations
- The scientific community should **educate itself and others** on robust experimental practices to maintain velocity without sacrificing innovation or good research practices.
- **Transparency** in LLM research is crucial and requires formal definitions of terminology, declaration of prompts, call parameters, and versioning.
- **Impact analysis sections** should disclose experimentation boundaries and areas of improvement or future work.
- **Measurement rigour** should explicitly account for statistical tests, number and type of metrics, languages evaluated, and classes of evaluators used.

## Ethical Implications
- There is a **decreased emphasis on ethics or appropriate research protocols**, raising concerns about the **experimental and ethical rigor** of LLM research.
- The **scientific community must prioritize good research practices**, despite the challenges posed by the current overload on authors and peer-reviewers.

## Conclusion
The study highlights a **tension between velocity and rigor** in LLM research, with conference checklists being effective but insufficient on their own. It calls for a **middle ground** where the scientific community can maintain innovation while upholding robust experimental and ethical standards. Future work should focus on **self-scrutiny, transparency, and addressing issues like synthetic data and contaminated benchmarks**.

## [summarize] 2026-08-14 11:04

**Q:** summarize: journal4.pdf

**A:** # Summary of Key Concepts and Ethical Considerations in Large Language Models

## Purpose

This summary outlines the technical foundations and ethical implications of large language models (LLMs), drawing on factual information from multiple sources.

## Methodology

- [Adopted Methodology] Large language models are trained using pretraining on extensive text corpora, followed by instruction tuning and preference alignment to improve performance and safety.
- [Adopted Methodology] Text generation is achieved through decoding techniques such as greedy decoding, beam search, and temperature sampling.
- [Adopted Methodology] Pretraining involves using cross-entropy loss and backpropagation to train models on predicting the next word in a sequence.
- [Adopted Methodology] Evaluation of LLMs is conducted using metrics such as perplexity and accuracy on specific tasks.

## Key Findings by Theme

### Technical Foundations

- Large language models are neural networks that predict the next word in a sequence based on previous words, generating text by sampling from a probability distribution.
- Decoding methods include greedy decoding (selecting the highest probability token at each step), beam search (considering multiple high-probability sequences), and temperature sampling (modifying the probability distribution using a temperature parameter).
- Temperature sampling allows for control over the diversity of generated text by adjusting the temperature parameter (low for more predictable text, high for more diverse text).

### Ethical Implications

- Large language models can generate false information (hallucinations), suggest unsafe actions, and reinforce harmful stereotypes or biases.
- They may also leak private information from their training data and be used for malicious purposes such as fraud, phishing, and disinformation.
- Emotional dependence on LLMs and the potential for psychological harm due to generated content are significant ethical concerns.
- Ethical safety issues include hallucinations, unsafe instructions, bias, stereotypes, misinformation, privacy violations, and copyright concerns.

## Ethical Considerations

- Ethical safety in large language models requires addressing issues such as hallucinations, bias, and privacy violations.
- Value sensitive design is essential to anticipate and mitigate potential harms.
- Researchers collaborate with Institutional Review Boards (IRBs) to ensure the safety of participants in studies involving LLMs.
- Datasheets or model cards are important tools for providing transparent and replicable information about training corpora.

## Conclusion

Large language models offer significant capabilities in natural language processing but also raise important ethical concerns. Their development and deployment must be guided by careful consideration of technical performance and ethical implications to ensure responsible use.
